#7308. OpenAI’s new business agents give brands a free way into the conversation it’s also selling as an ad

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The sponsored link had a good run. Its successor might talk back.

OpenAI certainly thinks so. Businesses can now claim a profile in ChatGPT through a new Business Hub, get verified and feed in product and company details. In the U.S. (for now), e-commerce and online services businesses can go a step further and publish a business agent – a separately labelled chat that speaks for the brand once a user wants to dig into a product. OpenAI’s vp of monetization Benji Shomair declined to share a timeline for further rollout.

He did, however, clarify that the business agent is the free, organic way into a format Digiday reported on in September, when OpenAI started testing it as a paid ad.

“Think of the business agent as the organic experience,” Shomair said during a press briefing. “The sponsored agent is the paid experience, but the agent that sits behind it should be the same.”

So, OpenAI has built two routes into the same conversation for brands, and only one of them costs money. One through ChatGPT’s organic recommendations, and the other via paid placements.

Shomair said early testing of sponsored agents had shown “promising results,” particularly in the depth of conversations between consumers and businesses.

“When a consumer chooses to engage more deeply with our business, there’s a high level of intent there,” he said.

Shomair described typical consumer use cases as working through product choices, weighing up size, colour and price before exploring related purchases. For businesses, that could result in turning an initial product enquiry into a bigger basket, and ultimately a deeper customer relationship.

When the shopper is ready to pay, the agent waves them off to the brand’s own site, because “it’s up to the business on how they want to close the transaction,” said Shomair.

Whatever got said before that point stays with OpenAI. Brands hoping to read back their agents’ transcripts get themes instead.

“The only insight provided today are the ones we sort of shared there initially, which are the themes of the conversations where you’re appearing,” Shomair said.

OpenAI keeps hold of the agent itself too. It’s built from the instructions and product feed a business supplies with no room for an in-house version. “We aren’t allowing folks to bring their own agents,” said Shomair.

The rest of the Business Hub sits under Insights, the the analytics dashboard-type product that Digiday reported on this week — the beta shows mentions, views and website clicks, plus discovery topics.

“Those are common themes in conversations where ChatGPT cites the business’s site,” an OpenAI spokesperson confirmed to Digiday. “Businesses don’t see individual prompts or chats. Share of voice isn’t part of this beta.”

How mentions are tracked depends on the type of business. For local businesses, a mention counts when ChatGPT shows a result for that location. For an online business, it counts when ChatGPT references the business’s website.

OpenAI declined to say how long mentions are tracked. An ad exec with access to Insights within the Business Hub did share that the chart within the feature shows everything within the past seven or 28 days.

Accuracast’s group CEO Farhad Divecha said that his team has been reliant on tools like Peec,.AI, Otterly.AI or Semrush to guesstimate brand visibility on ChatGPT as well as other AI platforms. But he noted that they are all using approximations from Google search data, third-party traffic estimators and their own data modeling tools, and none have access to original data from the source.

“Now that OpenAI is making their own mentions data available, we’re very interested to see how far off these tools were from the actual data from ChatGPT, and whether these tools will be able to adapt and incorporate the OpenAI data,” Divecha said. “If their estimation data was wildly erroneous, that will make us rethink paying for those tools.”

The implications extend beyond the accuracy of individual tools. OpenAI’s decision to share its own data could also shift the balance of power in the growing AI visibility industry. As Sonata Insights’ founder and chief analyst Debra Aho Williamson pointed out, the entire AI visibility (AEO/GEO) industry is built on third-party research (and some guesswork) about what helps a brand show up in an answer.

“The fact that OpenAI will now share insights itself will take some of the power away from the third-party companies,” she said. “But OpenAI isn’t going to share everything it knows, and ChatGPT is only one of several AI platforms, so demand for what the AI visibility companies offer will remain strong.”

Business Hub and agents are free for now but Shomair didn’t rule out a paid model in the future.

“In terms of where they develop in the future, I think it’ll depend on the directions that they go,” he said.

What none of it buys is a leg-up in ChatGPT’s recommendations. The core product needs to“maintain objectivity,” said Shomair, claiming a business “should not have an impact on how frequently it appears.”

That doesn’t mean the information is irrelevant to visibility though. Shomair said it could help ChatGPT surface a business in more relevant conversations since the platform would have a clearer picture of what that business offers.

#7307. Netflix embraces 35mm film in international campaign

“Find the Thing You Never Knew You Needed” consists of three new spots shot in different aspect ratios and a social series.

As U.S. consumers continue to cut back on subscriptions, including for streaming services, Netflix’s latest campaign emphasizes the quality of its vast content library in order to help it stand out in a crowded field. “Find the Thing You Never Knew You Needed” comes as company executives continue to put an emphasis on value and engagement following price hikes and lower projected ad revenue.

Created by Netflix’s in-house shop the Brand Creative Studio and agency Mother, the campaign features three spots shot on 35mm film using different aspect ratios. In “Axolotl,” which is shot for widescreen, an axolotl leverages its quadrupedalism to escape its tank and turn the TV back on after its owners leave in the middle of a “Bridgerton” marathon. “Dawn” follows a vampire who braves the rapidly rising sun to find a working TV in order to watch “Love Is Blind.” In “Hypersleep,” an astronaut who is the only one awake while his fellow shipmates are in cryosleep, wakes someone up in order to show them the “Soda Pop” scene from “KPop Demon Hunters.”

The spots were directed by Vania Heymann and Gal Muggia of the production company Iconoclast. The nature of the ads allows for the featured program to be swapped out depending on the market. In Brazil, the show featured in “Dawn” is crime drama “Os Donos Do Jogo,” or “Rulers of Fortune” in English.

Over the course of four episodes, the social series “Typecast” will feature four creatives and showcase their somewhat unexpected Netflix fixations. The series kicks off with fashion designer Willy Chavarria and his show of choice “Love on the Spectrum.” Other guests include musician Teezo Touchdown, makeup artist Isamaya Ffrench and Randy Savvy of The Compton Cowboys. The videos were filmed in 16mm to fit the feel of the overall campaign.

The campaign’s use of analog film is part of a recent trend that has seen advertisers looking to run counter to the rise of artificial intelligence. Stop-motion animation has been of particular interest, with brands such as Levi’s and Prada embracing the artform. Netflix’s spots, which feature custom-built sets, follow a similar vein of appealing to the desire for human-made advertising.

Netflix grew revenue 12% year over year to $12.9 billion in Q3, per an earnings release. The company lowered its expected ad revenue for the year from $3 billion to $2.7 billion, a drop it attributed to a slower-than-expected rollout of some advertising capabilities.

#7304. Future of TV Briefing: Inside creator Grace Wells’s 100-hour branded video production process

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Future of TV Briefing covers the latest in streaming and TV for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Wednesday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Future of TV Briefing looks at creator Grace Wells’s full production process, from initial brief to final delivery.

Picture the stereotypical sponsored video produced by a creator. You may be imagining someone holding up a product to their camera, hand behind the product to prevent their camera’s autofocus from blurring out said product, reciting a script about the product’s role in their everyday life and thanking the brand for partnering with them on this video.

Grace Wells (@gracewellsphoto) disproves that stereotype.

A creator who has attracted millions of followers across TikTok and Instagram, Wells represents how creator-produced branded videos have leveled up in production value. She spends, on average, 100 hours producing videos for brands and knocks out two to three branded videos per month. During Digiday’s recent Creator Strategies virtual event, she broke down her full production process, from initial brief to final delivery.

“It’s a very time-intensive process. I’m very big on pre-production, and that’s a question that I get a lot: is how much planning actually goes into these. There’s a lot of hours before I even get into the studio to film,” Wells said.

The first step in that process is joining a briefing call with the brand. Other creators may be satisfied with receiving a one-sheet telling them what the client is looking for. But Wells wants to have a conversation to understand the brand’s objective, its target audience and whether the video she is producing is meant to be tied to a larger marketing effort in case there are pre-existing brand assets or visual elements to incorporate.

“Because ultimately, I’m trying to create an asset that feels equally like me and like the brand, and that can be kind of a delicate balance to strike sometimes. Depending on how fleshed out the brief is and how much other content is being sourced for this particular campaign, I decide how many of those pre-existing elements I want to pull into my storyboarding and my ideation process,” said Wells.

Post-briefing call, Wells moves into her pre-production process. This stage can take roughly 30 hours. A pillar of Wells’s pre-production process is storyboarding. Every. Single. Shot.

“The perspective of the shot. How long that shot is going to last. Is there going to be voiceover or sound effects or text callouts that are happening?” said Wells.

She has adopted this extensive storyboarding process for two main reasons. First, it allows the brand to literally see what she intends to film and how she plans to edit it all together. Second, it allows Wells to head into filming on solid ground with minimal risk of the brand being dissatisfied when she hands in the final video and asking for reshoots.

“That can really mess me up and kind of create a domino effect in my workflow,” said Wells.

That domino effect is important to avoid because here’s the most crucial part of Wells’s production process to be aware of: She does everything by herself. That includes making custom newspapers in Photoshop (as she did for this recent video for Bayer), building sets, setting up lighting, prepping and styling food products and, of course, filming. Actually she effectively films two videos because the videos she produces for brands include the product showcase video as well as the behind the scenes of Wells making the product showcase video.

“The bulk of my time is spent on set filming, just because I am one person with two hands,” said Wells. She added, “every time I’m setting up a shot, I’m actually setting up two shots because I have the shot that I’m filming and then I have the behind the scenes that’s happening.”

A perk of filming solo is that it affords Wells the flexibility to film for as long as she wants, to take her time to experiment with shots and even to edit the video as she films it.

“I can film a shot then I can throw it into Premiere and edit it and that might inform decisions that I make about the following shot that I’m going to film,” Wells said.

Unsurprisingly then, post-production is the shortest stage of her process, typically taking around 20 hours to complete. She has her storyboards that effectively serve as a paper edit, so editing the video is a bit like coloring by numbers.

“Music is the very first thing that I choose, even before I’m storyboarding. So even decisions like that have been made at this point a very long time ago. And so it really is just putting the finishing touches on things,” she said.

This pre-production-heavy process helps Wells to avoid what can often be the bane of any creative client work: revisions.

“If there are any revisions, it’ll be simple things like, ‘Oh, actually let’s change the wording of this text,’ which is a very simple change, or ‘Let’s punch in on this so we can see details a little bit more.’ So I would say generally it’s like maybe three to four rounds of very small changes.”

“Exposure as a form of payment and with the intention of cutting into YouTube’s market share and cutting into the amount of views people are getting on YouTube — it’s just gross.”

359%: Percentage increase in daily AI retrieval of YouTube videos that have updated their descriptions.

495 million: Number of daily active users that Snap had at the end of Q3 2026.

21%: Percentage share of Gen Z survey respondents who think influencers rarely or never disclose paid deals.

$2.5 billion: Skydance’s DTC streaming revenue in Q3 2026.

#7303. Carnival gamifies cruise experience via interactive Twitch livestream

Streamer iJustine will take on a series of challenges voted on by viewers as she explores a digital Carnival Celebration ship.

Carnival is turning to Twitch to give consumers a unique look at what a trip aboard one of its vessels would entail at a time when the demographics of the average cruise passenger are in flux. The activation comes as the company reported record-high bookings for 2027 and a net income of $1.9 billion in the third quarter, an all-time high for the company. However, gross margin yields fell 1.3%  due to high fueling costs, per an earnings release.

Cruising has become more popular than ever, with a record 22.4 million Americans expected to take a cruise in 2027, up 3.6% year over year, per AAA research. The pre-pandemic total for American cruise travelers was 14.2 million. While the vacation choice is stereotypically associated with older demographics, the average age of an American cruiser is 45.8, and millennials and Gen Z travelers are increasingly drawn to the travel method due to lower costs and value-oriented packages, according to AAA research.

The livestream will feature a custom-built game in which iJustine, who has nearly 55,000 followers on Twitch at press time, will tackle challenges on the simulated Carnival Celebration. The ship’s spaces and activities, as well as destinations from the brand’s Paradise Destinations collection, will be part of the gameplay. Along with input from the audience, Weigand will help keep energy high by introducing challenges, passing on requests from viewers and offering community instructions. iJustine, who will be playing live onboard, will work to earn her cruise director badge and a Fun Meter will track energy levels.The game is over once the meter hits zero.

To encourage participation, audience members will have the chance to win a cruise vacation. The full livestream will be posted to Instagram and TikTok the day after the event so fans can catch up on what they missed. The game will not be made available to the public after the stream.

“We’re constantly looking for new ways to push the boundaries on how we connect with consumers, and Play Carnival takes that thinking to a whole new level,” said Michael Sotelo, vice president of brand, product and social media at Carnival Cruise Line, in press materials.

The effort falls under the “Find Your Fun Again” campaign, which encourages consumers to ditch the stress and monotony of everyday life and find joy aboard a Carnival ship. Actor Nick Offerman took center stage in a spot in which he goes on vacation after smashing his phone due to receiving an overload of notifications.

Amazon-owned platform Twitch has become a go-to for brands looking to experiment with connecting with young consumers. Last year, E.l.f. Cosmetics became the first brand to test Twitch’s livestream shopping ads.

#7300. TikTok Shop launches a paid premium tier for 1M of its top U.S. shoppers

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The story was first exclusively published by Digiday sibling Modern Retail.

TikTok Shop has officially launched a paid membership pilot in the U.S. called TikTok Shop Plus in time for the competitive holiday season.

Over the weekend, TikTok Shop began offering the service to more than 1 million people it has identified as its most loyal U.S. customers. TikTok Shop Plus costs $9.99 a month after a 30-day free trial, according to TikTok Shop. It includes perks such as expanded free shipping, exclusive discounts, coupon bundles and instant refunds.

The program is TikTok Shop’s latest effort to grow retail sales in the U.S. as live commerce takes off outside of Asia. TikTok Shop launched in the U.S. in 2023 and now represents about 20% of the social-commerce market in the country, per Capital One Shopping. With TikTok Shop Plus, the platform is offering extra perks similar to other paid membership programs like Amazon Prime ($14.99 a month) and Walmart+ ($12.95 a month). Business Insider first reported that TikTok Shop was testing a version of the program in July.

“TikTok Shop Plus is currently being piloted with an expanding group of eligible U.S. users,” Patrick Nommensen, president of strategic initiatives at TikTok Shop, said in a statement to Modern Retail. “As we gradually scale access to the pilot, we’ll continue to gather feedback and refine the experience, with the goal of delivering more value and convenience to shoppers.”

TikTok Shop Plus members have access to multiple perks. First, they can get free shipping on eligible items marked with a “Plus” badge on the product page. Plus members also receive an extra 10% off select products. In addition, each month, Plus members will get three coupons for a range of products. The deals are: $5 off with no minimum spend, $10 off orders of $59 or more, and $30 off orders of $119 or more.

What’s more, for qualified products, members can get an instant refund (up to two times per month), issued as store credit to their TikTok Shop balance before they drop off the return. For TikTok Shop’s Black Friday and Cyber Monday period — which kicks off Wednesday — TikTok Shop Plus members will receive additional exclusive coupons and deeper discounts on select items. The app will display specific offers.

TikTok Shop has high hopes for the holidays this year. Notably, 2026 will be the first holiday season when big names like Best Buy, Ulta Beauty and Sephora will be selling products on TikTok Shop, meaning that shoppers will have more major retail players to choose from than in any previous year. And, this year, shoppers can browse and buy holiday gifts with the help of TikTok Shop’s new AI assistant. Shoppers can also now buy directly in TikTok’s For You feed, without leaving the app.

Nearly a third of U.S. TikTok users expect to buy a gift this Black Friday, per TikTok Shop data. Like previous years, live shopping and limited-edition products will be front and center in TikTok Shop’s holiday strategy. During the 2025 four-day Black Friday and Cyber Monday weekend, TikTok Shop’s sales surpassed $500 million, and sellers hosting livestreams saw 84% year-over-year sales growth across more than 760,000 sessions, per a press release.

Americans spend about $43 million per day shopping on TikTok, according to Capital One Shopping. Many of these purchases are health and beauty products, but the platform continues to expand into new categories every year, including big-ticket items like comforters and above-ground pools. Today, TikTok Shop is becoming so crucial to some brands’ businesses that many, including Harry’s and Stila Cosmetics, are creating dedicated positions for heads of TikTok Shop.

TikTok Shop U.S. is now home to retail companies of various sizes, including Vera Bradley, True Religion, Beachwaver and Mellow Sleep. Last month, PF Global’s TikTok’s Economic Impact Report found that economic activity on TikTok helped generate $81 billion in GDP for U.S. businesses in 2025, and that 54 million Americans purchased a product after watching a TikTok video last year. Earlier this month, TechCrunch reported news of a standalone TikTok Shop app in select markets like Singapore, Malaysia and Thailand.

“TikTok Shop is no longer experimental,” Nommensen said at a summit in October. “It’s now a key component to these brands’ e-commerce strategy, and we’re really seeing that shift accelerate as time goes by.”

#7298. Maybelline updates ‘Maybe’ tagline as beauty perceptions evolve

Miley Cyrus is leading a new global brand platform promoting changes meant to be more inclusive and empowering for women.

Maybelline is reworking one of its signature brand assets as it tries to adapt to an evolving landscape for beauty and cosmetics. The L'Oréal-owned marketer’s “Maybe she’s born with it” tagline became a fixture of its advertising beginning in 1991, but consumers in 2026 are applying makeup for different reasons.

The company has attempted to modernize “Maybe” in the past. It was at one point dropped for the largely forgotten “Make It Happen” but eventually brought back. Maybelline revamped the “Maybe” tagline for the TikTok era two years ago. The latest overhaul appears more substantial, changing the underlying meaning of the copy, and is described in the release as permanent.

Over half of surveyed global respondents today see beauty as a way to build confidence compared to 26% who see it as a means of looking more attractive to other people or appearing younger for longer, per McKinsey findings cited by Maybelline. The suggestion is that makeup can be something less superficial and more empowering and inclusive.

Tweaks to the marketer’s tagline try to recognize these trends by adjusting the phrasing to sound less like a question being asked about a single woman’s look. Instead, the new “Maybe” is positioned as a declaration of the possibilities all women can achieve by leveraging makeup as a form of self-expression.

“With 'Maybe.', we're building on our legacy and inviting women everywhere, at every stage of life, to explore the possibilities within them,” said Maybelline Global Brand President Sandrine Jolly in a statement.

Maybelline enlisted Cyrus, an ambassador who’s previously voiced the “Maybe” tagline, to lead its new “The Beauty of Infinite Possibility” platform. Cyrus was selected for the effort because her own career has entailed a constantly shifting identity, moving from child star to hard-partying club song purveyor and then back to her country roots.

Cyrus released her latest album, “Bass Persuades,” in September, which could draw additional interest to the Maybelline partnership. “Bass Persuades” sees Cyrus again dabbling in the dance pop and electronic music genres.

Products featured in the campaign include Maybelline’s Serum Lipstick, Lash Sensational Sky High Mascara and Instant Eraser Concealer. Maybelline will partner with additional women in the worlds of music and elsewhere to share their stories of using makeup as a form of expression and reinvention.

Consumer products, the division of L'Oréal that Maybelline sits under, grew 4.3% on a like-for-like basis in Q3, lagging other segments like dermatological beauty and professional products.

#7297. Mars wants to future proof impulse buys as AI is poised to take over more grocery runs

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

When AI does the grocery shopping, what happens to the impulse snack purchase at checkout? That’s the question Mars is grappling with.

The conglomerate, which owns snack brands like Dove chocolate, M&Ms and Pop-Tarts, is hedging its bets against an agentic future. Neil Reynolds, global chief customer officer at Mars, doesn’t expect AI to kill the in-store checkout lane, but he expects that agentic shopping assistants will take over more of the weekly grocery run. At least that’s the case based on Mars’ recent future of shopping and retail report penned in partnership with Kantar and Synthesis.

Digiday caught up with Reynolds to discuss how Mars is trying to sell products to AI, the upshot of AI visibility and the future of impulse purchases in an agentic landscape.

This interview has been lightly edited for clarity.

For us, the road map sits against those two parallel parts… how can we build the right capabilities internally to make sure our categories and our brand really shine in agentic commerce. That means, how do we make sure our brands are discoverable by the LLMs and how do we make sure that once those brands are discovered, that they convert through to purchase? The second milestone plan, or the second road map, is around, how do we start to step into building these experiential, immersive retail destinations for our stores? How do we partner with our retailers to think about, in effect, creating different cultural environments, and building a different experience when you step into a store?

The first thing we’re trying to do is sort of get some real data around what’s the start point for how our categories and how our brands show up. What that looks like for us is trying to get data through partnerships with emerging tech companies to get a start point for how we can show up in the LLM. What’s the data that, in effect, is showing up and where’s that data being sourced from?

Part of this genuinely is about connectivity between the teams. We’re setting up what we call One Demand performance squads — which are, again, how do you get the brand manager next to the insights person next to the key account manager next to the category manager, and the agency partner?

Winning this consumer journey through effortless, personalized and experiential, will be within the Publicis functionality or contract as well now. Our historical relationships are more been around media and creative, and what we’ve now got with Publicis is an end-to-end agency partner who can also support us with commerce. We can unlock some of the data that they have. They talk about connected ID being part of their offer, and then we can ideate to build those effortless, personalized experiential shopper experiences.

Back in the day, we would have a salesperson from Mars going to see a commercial buyer from a retailer. But we’ve quite quickly worked out that if we want to be strategic partners, we need to bring more people to that conversation. For example, how can we have a tech-to-tech conversation. Why is our data team not talking to the data team at Walmart?

We have ongoing conversations with many of the key leading retailers around, call it “store of the future initiatives” — and how we can ideate around continuing to create even more immersive and engaging shopping experiences for snacking in their stores.

#7295. How a sports betting creator drove 200 million short-form views of his podcast

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of an ongoing series examining successful creator business strategies. More from the series →

After some trial and error, Zach “Mystic Zach” Hirsch has determined how to toe the content line. The sports betting analyst and commentator has nearly 2 million Instagram followers, and a Draft Kings-sponsored podcast, Double Coverage, that boasts millions of views across YouTube and TikTok.

But not long ago, one of Hirsch’s biggest videos (both in terms of views and in terms of guests, as it was an interview with fellow creator-turned-boxer Jake Paul) was taken off YouTube, reportedly because it was sponsored by an offshore betting company Hirsch wouldn’t name, rather than one of the more mainstream platforms like Draft Kings, which is vetted by Google and available state-by-state depending on local gambling laws. The offshore sportsbooks exist in a sort of legal gray area — they’re unlicensed in the U.S., but federal law targets the operators and payment processors rather than the bettors themselves. Still, the takedown threw his content approach into relief and forced him to take a step back.

“That was really scary,” said Hirsch. He said the company wasn’t an approved Google vendor, which seemingly prompted the strike, but didn’t provide details.

This spring, Hirsch found that partner in Draft Kings, which he calls an “anchor sponsor.” There’s exclusivity, but only in the category of sportsbooks and prediction markets, so he’s free to get other partnerships, and the increased social acceptance of legal betting brands has made it fairly easy for him to find new partners, including direct-to-consumer telehealth and online pharmacy brand Hims. He did not say how much his partnerships are worth — or how long they will last.

“Sports betting hasn’t been a notable flag for brands when they vet creators,” said Mat Micheli, co-CEO and co-founder of Viral Nation. “Betting is now deeply ingrained in the culture of the brands that deem it important.”

However, Micheli stressed the importance of partnering with licensed, regulated vendors to avoid what happened to Hirsch last year.

“There are regulated multinational operators in other parts of the world that are legitimate and licensed, just not in the United States. For American creators speaking to an American audience, the standard has to be simple: you only promote something that is lawful to use in your audience’s own country. That’s the line that separates a brand-safe partnership from a risky one,” he said.

Hirsch’s creator career is deeply entwined with the controversial world of sports betting. He signed a contract with BetOnline as soon as he turned 18, and spent nearly five years with them, co-hosting a sports prediction podcast with former NFL player Adam “Pacman” Jones. At the time, however, he was just a paid host, and not involved in the show’s day-to-day production or marketing efforts.

Hirsch decided he wanted to take a more hands-on approach for his next podcast endeavor.

“I saw a lot of soft spots and inconsistencies that could be improved, especially as a Gen Zer,” he said. The biggest soft spot for him was the lack of reliance on other social media platforms to drive discovery. “Eighty-five percent of Gen Z sports podcast listeners are introduced through social media, which is clips,” Hirsch said, referencing a 2024 Edison Research report.

He leveraged his existing connections in the professional sports and entertainment worlds, which he built with a different podcast, Kiss and Myst, co-hosted by rap legend Jadakiss (Hirsh said the podcast was sponsored by World Star Hiphop for seven figures). These connections helped ensure Double Coverage would have an impressive slate of guests, from WWE wrestler Cody Rhodes to boxing legend Mike Tyson, as well as a regular slate of NFL players and UFC fighters, and leaned hard into circulating clips on TikTok and YouTube.

“We’ve had over 200 million views on short-form content since the NFL season began,” Hirsh said. Since July, the podcast has seen steady, impressive growth, with 151 million cumulative views on TikTok, and 43.7 million on TikTok, per the platform’s statistics shared with Digiday.

“I’ve gone all in on short-form content and identifying what resonates most,” Hirsh said. That includes shaving off a half-second or word to make the clip more punchy and hiring a professional designer for thumbnails. In its second season, Hirsch pivoted to make the podcast more fast-paced so it can easily be cut up into clips.

“There’s no room to breathe; it’s like you’re watching one continuous TikTok clip, which is what resonates with our audience,” he said.

For Hirsh, short-form video is not just a promotional channel; it shapes how the show is made. He says the team edits clips tightly, invests in thumbnails, and has made the second season faster-paced so moments can be cut for social platforms.

Double Coverage is also looking beyond the screen, with plans for more experiential programming and in-person events. Hirsh said his ability to “set up anywhere, travel anywhere, fit into any crowd” could help make that possible. He doesn’t have an official NFL partnership, but he says he is, “in talks” and plans on having a “massive media day presence” for the second year in a row.

“I’d love to get 100 million views a week on short-form content and get over a million views a podcast episode,” he said. “I’d like to have four anchor sponsors that believe in me and are committed long term that I can build around.”

Google is moving on two fronts: working up the U.K’s Competition and Markets Authority-mandated search opt-out publishers have long sought, while enhancing AI Overviews in ways that could make opting out less appealing.

Major AI companies are backing a new AI training opt-out standard proposal for publishers, but it exposes deeper tensions over AI scraping.

Instagram wants to pull long-form creators away from YouTube, but is it offering enough? Experts weigh in on the platform’s pivot.

#7276. Senior ML Engineer - Malware Detection (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is a global remote-first company. We are driven by our principles: do the right thing, employees first, we are remote first, and we deliver high-volume, low-cost Linux infrastructure and security products that help companies to increase the efficiency of their operations. Every person on our team supports each other and does what we can to ensure we all are successful.

Check out our website for more information: https://cloudlinux.com/

Imunify360 is CloudLinux's security suite for Linux web servers: real-time and background malware scanning, automated cleanup, a WAF, runtime PHP protection (Proactive Defense), a database malware scanner, and IP reputation. It runs on hosting providers' shared, VPS, and dedicated servers, so new web-malware campaigns land on our fleet early.

We sit on one of the largest labeled corpora of live web-server malware in the industry: files, verdicts, cleanup outcomes, and analyst decisions collected from production hosting servers every day. It already drives our signatures, heuristics, and ML models, and there is far more to get out of it. We are looking for a Senior ML Engineer who will turn that data into detection that ships — classifiers, LLM-assisted triage and generated signatures — and keep exploring new ML/LLM methods against it, measured by the product's false-negative and false-positive rates, not by notebook accuracy.

Our dataset focuses on web malware — PHP, JavaScript, HTML phishing kits, infected WordPress and Joomla plugins and themes, database injections — with the context around each file.

The goal is 10x more malware detected at the same or lower false-positive rate. Hand-written rules and faster pipelines will not get there; models trained on our data might.

You get an experimentation track, GPUs, and a direct line to leadership; in return, you bring numbers, not opinions.

You own the path from raw samples to a model in production, and the numbers that prove it helped.

#7275. X Previews Labels That Tell Users Why Its AI System Flagged Their Posts

X’s AI-powered content moderation system once mistakenly labeled a post featuring an image of a SpaceX rocket launch as “adult content.” No one, including the author of the post, could see why.

That’s the problem Michael O’Herlihy, a Twitter veteran who recently rejoined X as head of safety, wants to fix.

In the future, X’s content moderation systems may show their decisioning process, he suggested at the Brand & AI Safety Summit in New York on Tuesday (Nov. 10). There, O’Herlihy previewed designs for labels displayed beneath content on X that explain what its AI detected in a post, detail how exactly the post’s reach was limited after being flagged, and allow users to appeal the decision.

#7271. Senior ML Engineer - Email Security (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is a global remote-first company. We are driven by our principles: do the right thing, employees first, we are remote first, and we deliver high-volume, low-cost Linux infrastructure and security products that help companies to increase the efficiency of their operations. Every person on our team supports each other and does what we can to ensure we all are successful.

Check out our website for more information: https://cloudlinux.com/

CloudLinux Inc. develops the Imunify family of security products, designed to protect hosting environments from a wide range of cyber threats. One of these solutions is Imunify Email, an email security product that protects hosting servers against spam, phishing, and other email-based threats.

Imunify Email filters mail on about 5,000 hosting servers. Today the filter is rspamd with rules and a scoring model we train in-house. We are replacing it with a transformer we trained ourselves that runs on the customer's own CPU in under 100 MB and already beats rspamd at every operating point we measured. We are looking for a Senior Machine Learning Engineer to join our team and work alongside the engineer currently responsible for both the existing spam-filtering pipeline and the new ML model. Together, you will drive the development, improvement, and production rollout of our email security technology.

We’re looking for a Data API Engineer to join our Data API Team and help improve the feature set, reliability and performance of our Data API, based on the PostgREST project.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Work on PostgREST’s PostgreSQL driver (based on Hasql) and remove bottlenecks and improve it’s performance.

Work on PostgREST development of new features and bug fixes, aligned towards the goal of a multitenant Data API.

Resolve incidents and escalated support issues, while also improving observability and performance.

Collaborate cross-functionally with other teams to debug and solve integration issues with the Supabase platform.

Strong Haskell skills with at least 3 years of experience.

Solid knowledge of SQL and plpgsql scripting in PostgreSQL.

Strong track record of contributing to and engaging with open-source projects. Bonus points if you’ve maintained an open source project.

Strong experience with providing technical support to users with varying levels of technical expertise.

Experience with debugging applications under the Linux operating system.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

The current landscape of tools for uploading, storing, processing and delivering assets is complicated. #7243. Container Engineer at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth. More than 9 million developers and 250,000+ customers now build on Supabase, and the majority of new databases on the platform are created by AI app builders — so the systems underneath have to stay reliable while they grow fast.

Container Engineering (ConEng) owns the internal container platform that runs Supabase's multi-tenant, multi-region services — the managed Kubernetes platform that other engineering teams build and deploy on top of. We own cluster lifecycle and cluster classes, multi-cluster and multi-region topology, capacity, scheduling, isolation and runtime safety, the higher-level platform APIs, the Kubernetes deployment contract and Argo semantics, rollout primitives, and runtime configuration and secret injection.

Your job is to make the platform itself trustworthy, observable, and cheap to operate at scale — and to raise the reliability bar for everyone who depends on it. This is not a gatekeeper role. You'll make the reliable path the easy path: building the automation and platform APIs that let product teams run safely by default, instrumenting what we operate, and making sure that when something breaks we detect it and recover fast.

Tooling & ecosystem — deep knowledge of the container and orchestration landscape (Kubernetes, containerd/OCI, the CNCF ecosystem) and a clear point of view on how to compose it.

Software engineering — designing and building the platform's automation, controllers, and internal tooling as production code, including solutions that don't come pre-shaped by an existing tool.

Operational / SRE practice — the discipline (SLOs, error budgets, incident response, ORRs) that runs the platform at scale as a predictable one.

We expect you to be genuinely deep in at least one of these, and many of our strongest candidates are deep in two (usually leaning toward one). Bringing real strength across all three is rare and exceptional — and even then, no one is equally strong in all three at once, so you'd focus where you're most needed at any given time. Different profiles are welcome here; tell us which of these is your center of gravity.

Regardless of the skill set, all team members carry a share of the team's on-call rota, responding to platform incidents and driving them to resolution and follow-up.

Define SLIs/SLOs and error budgets for the container platform itself, and build error-budget policies that actually gate platform changes.

Own the Operational Readiness Review (ORR) process for new platform capabilities and major changes.

Design and track metrics for platform uptime, and increase observability into the platform by capturing the right metrics, logs, and traces across the platform layers.

Drive the incident-to-improvement pipeline: facilitate blameless postmortems, connect findings to readiness gaps, spot repeat failure patterns, and push through systemic fixes.

Build and maintain the platform's automation, controllers, operators, and internal tooling — writing production code and shipping it, not just wiring together existing tools.

Design and implement the platform-side CI/CD and rollout primitives that speed up platform deployments while working within proper change and release management.

Implement and maintain security automation for the platform: intrusion detection, automated remediation, and patch management.

Identify and quantify operational toil, and build the automation that removes it rather than just documenting it.

Contribute to the platform's continual testing automation — the test suites and pipelines that validate platform releases are fit for purpose before they reach tenants.

Act as a platform reliability and architecture expert — contribute to failure-mode analysis, dependency mapping, and resilience design for multi-tenant container workloads.

Evaluate and evolve the platform's tooling ecosystem (orchestration, runtime, networking, isolation, and observability stack) with a clear, opinionated view on trade-offs.

Improve system architecture to reduce infrastructure cost while balancing security, reliability, and performance across tenants.

Guide teams building on the platform to design sustainable on-call practices for their own services: alert quality, escalation paths, runbook coverage, and noise reduction.

Have 5+ years in platform engineering, container/Kubernetes platform operations, SRE, or production infrastructure, and have operated production systems at scale and carried on-call for them.

Are genuinely deep in at least one — ideally two — of the three strength areas below (and tell us which is your center of gravity):

Tooling & ecosystem — know the container/orchestration landscape well: Kubernetes, containerd and the OCI/CRI standards, and the surrounding CNCF ecosystem (e.g. Argo CD/Argo Rollouts for GitOps and progressive delivery; Cilium/eBPF networking and the Gateway API; Karpenter/KEDA-style autoscaling; and workload-isolation runtimes such as gVisor or Kata Containers). You reason about trade-offs rather than reaching for defaults.

Software engineering — write real production code (Go and/or a comparable systems language) and can build controllers, operators, and platform APIs — stepping back to the underlying problem and building the right solution when no off-the-shelf tool fits.

Operational / SRE practice — are fluent in SLOs, error budgets, and operational KPIs; have led incident response and blameless postmortems; and have driven down MTTD/MTTR for systems you operated.

Have worked with large-scale multi-tenant systems and understand isolation as a spectrum — from namespaces, RBAC, network policies, and resource quotas through to stronger primitives such as virtual clusters (e.g. vCluster) or VM-class runtimes.

Operate confidently on AWS (multiple accounts, IAM, VPC) and are comfortable with infrastructure-as-code (Pulumi preferred; Terraform/CDK also fine) and Kubernetes.

Script and automate to eliminate toil rather than absorb it.

Communicate clearly with both infrastructure specialists and product engineers, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and iterating toward better systems over time.

Experience building and operating a multi-tenant Kubernetes platform that internal teams deploy onto.

Familiarity with policy engines (Kyverno, or OPA/Gatekeeper) and cost-attribution tooling (OpenCost/Kubecost) for per-tenant chargeback/showback.

Experience with managed database platforms or Postgres.

Familiarity with OpenTelemetry, Prometheus/VictoriaMetrics, Grafana, or similar observability tooling.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Although we are headquartered in Gibraltar, this is a full time, 100% remote position Work from anywhere!

Xapo's product suite has grown from a Bitcoin vault into a full member proposition: BTC and USD accounts that pay interest, a global card with Bitcoin cashback, Bitcoin-backed loans, a growing suite of BTC wealth and credit products, and access to stocks, ETFs, and stablecoins, all inside one regulated relationship.

Xapo is a bank, and a bank earns on the money its members choose to hold with it. Each of those products shipped well. What hasn't yet existed is a single owner of the economics of the customer relationship: one person accountable for winning the right members, growing what they hold with us, and keeping them, and for the balance-sheet products that make that possible.

The Chief Customer Officer is a new role at the top of the house, reporting to the CEO. You will run the stream that grows what members hold with Xapo, and you will own the Customer P&L, retail and institutional. It brings growth, client coverage, product management, and product design under one leader who is judged on customer contribution, the assets attracted and kept, and the members who stay. This is a customer-focused role in the most commercial sense: what a member earns from us, not the feature we shipped, is the centre of every decision.

The measure of success is a Customer P&L that grows on what members hold and keep: LTV:CAC improving, approved-to-paid rising, churn falling, and customer contribution the business can plan against.

This is a banking role, not a requirements-gathering role, a marketing role, or a role you can run from a dashboard. Products are priced on the balance sheet and judged on the money they attract and keep.

This part matters as much as the mandate. The known failure mode for this role, in any company, is a CCO with a polished customer-insight deck and no ownership of the number. That is disqualifying here.

Day 30: The Customer P&L baseline is documented across retail and institutional: customer contribution, LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, net paid-member growth, and assets attracted and kept. You have formed your own independent view of where members are won, lost, and under-served, and shared it with the CEO and Board.Day 60: The unified Customer operating model is live: one set of key results across Growth, Client Coverage, Mission PMs, and Product Design, read weekly from one source. Every mission PM owns a product's contribution and its price, and the roadmap is ranked on what it earns, built with Finance and the line leads.Day 90: At least one measurable improvement in LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, or net new AUM flow is live and attributable to a change you made, not a feature ship, an outcome moved. Every cell in your line has defined key results and an owner who explains the number every week.

Shape the FutureImprove lives through cutting-edge technology, work 100% remotely from anywhere in the world.

Great work-life balanceBuild amazing things with a balance of autonomy and collaborative teamwork. Set your own work schedule and make use of a flexible PTO plan when you need to recharge.

Expect ExcellenceCollaborate, learn, and grow with a high-performance team. Learn how you learn best - from books to conferences, you’ll get a yearly budget for your individual learning and development goals.

#7218. The Washington Post Nears Breakeven After ‘Hundreds and Hundreds of Millions’ in Losses

This story was originally published in On Background with Mark Stenberg, a free, weekly newsletter that explores the key themes shaping the media industry. You can sign up for it here.

After years of steep losses, layoffs, and leadership churn, The Washington Post is within striking distance of breaking even financially, according to executive editor Matt Murray.

In a conversation at the Web Summit conference in Lisbon, Murray argued that the publisher is poised to turn a corner in 2027. While that won’t be easy, The Post has recently begun rebuilding its commercial momentum, stacking up a handful of revenue wins alongside a string of editorial scoops.

In the third quarter, direct advertising sales at The Post notched their strongest year-over-year growth since 2021, while programmatic revenue was up 48% year over year as of September, according to a company spokesperson.

Meanwhile, the newsroom is hiring again, adding James Politi from the Financial Times, Nancy Cook from Bloomberg, and Ian Ward from Politico, among other additions.

The Post is also relaunching its Ben Bradlee award, an internal designation that honors the work of an outstanding reporter. On Tuesday, the award will go to Hannah Natanson, whose home the FBI raided in January, according to Murray. The Post will also be recognizing Heidi Levine, the photojournalist who was attacked by Israeli settlers in the occupied West Bank last month.

In the Q&A below, Murray reflects on five years of turmoil at The Post, the first few months of the D’Onofrio era, and his plan to own post-Trump Washington.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

Mark Stenberg: How did election night go?

Matt Murray: We had a good night, and we wanted and needed that. This whole year has been about getting the layoffs behind us, but more than that, getting five years of turmoil behind us. The layoffs were terrible, but they were a kind of climax to a long stretch of buyouts and staff turnover. We hoped they would punctuate it, and that seems to be what happened. The rest of the year has been about stability, core coverage, and breaking scoops.

Mark: You were recruited to The Post by Will Lewis, who left in February after much controversy. How does the leadership style of his successor, Jeff D’Onofrio, stack up?

Matt: I’m cautious here. Will is a friend and somebody I’ve worked with for a long, long time, so I don’t want to make too much of a contrast. But we had some deep-seated challenges in how we were organized and how we understood our audience. We didn’t have great data. When I arrived two and a half years ago, too much of the newsroom was still oriented toward print. We had more foundational work to do than people realized. Jeff brings a low-drama, data-oriented focus on fundamentals. He’s very calm. He’s about the journalism, but he knows he’s not a journalist. He’s a business guy, and he relies on us. We haven’t had any clashes about that.

Matt: I’ll be honest. We had years of losses, and steepening losses. That’s the truth. We’re a private company and don’t share the figures, but it was hundreds and hundreds of millions of dollars. Now we’re within striking distance of break-even, and I expect next year will be our best year in seven years. We’ve done the right-sizing we needed, which was tough.

FOR Scoops, Sharp Analysis, and Insider Context

Mark: Layoffs might reduce costs, but where do you see growth coming from?

Matt: The Post has got to be about Washington, and I didn’t think we were dominant enough there in reach and comprehensiveness. We’ve had real successes this year: the ballroom, the Kennedy Center, the monuments. But how do we own post-Trump Washington? The whole city has been oriented to Trump for 10 years, and he’s still the center of the action. But last week was about millennials and Gen Z coming to the fore. I’m an older guy, and I’m trying to understand the world through their eyes. This is the first time in my lifetime that the boomers are receding and the next generations are rising. That’s the audience we want for news.

Mark: What editorial changes are you making that will help drive new business?

Matt: One thing we’re trying to do better is understand franchises. We suspended our daily podcast because costs overran the revenue, but now we can sell on the ad side, including through a partnership with Acast. The Post talked for years about [advice columnist] Carolyn Hax having a podcast. She has one now, and it’s taking off quickly. Personal finance reporter Michelle Singletary has one too. We’re reviving a version of the daily podcast, and we have at least half a dozen people for whom we can find that sweet spot of newsletter, podcast, and writing.

Mark: People poke at The Post for not acquiring properties the way The Times does, which has recently set its sights on Letterboxd. How do you see that?

Matt: The Times deserves enormous credit for how they reoriented the entire company and how it interacted with the newsroom. It took them many years. In hindsight, it seems inevitable, but it wasn’t. Our company struggled during that decade. We had successes in the more open internet age, like Facebook partnerships, and of course we had a Trump bump. We went from something like 35,000 subscriptions before Trump to 3 million. In the long run, we probably need our version of that model, with the journalism at the center. But we’re still getting the Post itself where it needs to be.

City Casting Call (EXCLUSIVE): The cofounder and chief executive of City Cast, David Plotz, is stepping away from the nationwide network of city-based podcasts after six years, according to the company. As part of the transition, the publisher is naming cofounder and former chief creative officer Andi McDaniel as its new chief executive. Last October, I wrote about the recalibration afoot at City Cast, which was shuttering certain markets, expanding others, and tweaking its editorial strategy. At the time, it was generating around $5 million but had not yet reached profitability.

Continued: The leadership change comes at an intriguing moment: City Cast is backed by Graham Holdings, which also owns Slate. In late October, Slate named its editor in chief, Hillary Frey, as its new CEO, replacing Dan Check; then, last week, it laid off a small number of staff, according to an inside source. It would appear that some end-of-year housekeeping is underway at Graham, which—after selling The Washington Post to Jeff Bezos in 2013—has struggled with some of its media holdings. In 2023, after paying $348 million for the media company Leaf Group, which itself would shutter in 2025, Graham Holdings president and CEO Timothy J. O’Shaughnessy admitted to investors that they had “overpaid” for the asset. “I screwed this one up,” he wrote in a shareholders letter at the time.

AP Up (EXCLUSIVE): The Associated Press had a big week last week. In addition to penning a content licensing agreement with Anthropic, the first of its kind for the artificial intelligence firm, the publisher also grew elections revenue by more than 80% compared to the 2022 midterms, according to the company. The AP saw its highest traffic since the 2024 election, with over 23 million pageviews, a sixfold increase over election night in 2022.

Multimedia Brew: The New York Times veteran Nina Lassam joined Morning Brew Inc. as its first senior vice president of multimedia on Wednesday, MBI chief executive Robert Dippell shared on LinkedIn on Tuesday. Lassam worked at The Times for nearly a decade, where she helped build out its now robust audio and video offerings. The hire underscores just how seriously Morning Brew Inc. is taking its podcast, social video, and creator franchise efforts.

In our conversation, Dreyfus shared a number of surprising elements, including the unlikely way in which the OpenAI deal originated, what Le Monde did with the licensing revenue it gained from the deal, and why it has avoided the fallout from Google Zero.

Mark Stenberg: Le Monde was the first French publisher to sign with OpenAI. How did that happen?

Louis Dreyfus: In fall 2023, I saw that Sam Altman was speaking at Station F, so I went. When they took questions from the room, I raised my hand and said: “I’m the CEO of Le Monde. Seventy-five percent of my revenue comes from readers. You need reliable data, and you give it to users for free. If you want Le Monde to keep publishing reliable news, we need to negotiate.” He said he knew Le Monde and would be happy to. Three months later, we had a deal.

Mark: What have the partnerships actually delivered?

Louis: First, a significant and sustainable revenue stream at a time when advertising in Western Europe is slow. Second, a new audience, as the readers click through far more than people coming from Facebook, and they convert to subscriptions. And I decided to redistribute a share of the licensing revenue from the deal to the newsroom, so every journalist at Le Monde gets an annual bonus from it.

Mark: Anthropic has largely avoided these kinds of deals. Have you spoken with them?

Louis: Our last conversation was almost a year ago. They said it was too early and that their focus was B2B, not general news. I believe that will change, though, as they prepare to go public and investors will ask whether their data is stolen or legitimate. The more surprising holdout is Mistral, a French unicorn backed by the French government that refuses to sign a licensing deal.

Mark: Google rolled out AI Overviews in France this summer. What impact have you seen?

Louis: It launched in mid-July, and we haven’t seen a drop in audience. I was in London last week, and British publishers said the same about general news. They lost traffic on evergreen content, food, automotive, and consumer electronics.

You work iteratively. Your process embodies a “ship and iterate” mentality. You value the quick pace of a growing startup like Clerk.

#7176. Senior Product Builder - Hub at Camunda

Camunda is the enterprise platform for agentic orchestration, enabling organizations to coordinate AI agents, people, and systems across complex, end-to-end business processes. With built-in governance, auditability, and human oversight, Camunda gives enterprises the control they need to move AI from pilots to production — safely and at scale. Trusted by over 700 organizations worldwide, including 9 of top 10 US banks, Camunda helps enterprises boost operational efficiency, accelerate time-to-value, and deliver better customer experiences.Fully remote and global, we are in the middle of something bigger: transforming into an AI-first organisation, built on our own platform. We use Agentic AI to automate, orchestrate intelligent processes, and elevate human contribution across every team.Named GP Bullhound’s Top 100 Next Unicorn list, 2025 Great Place to Work certified. Visionary in 2025 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies. ranked 3rd in Flexa's 2026 Most Flexible Companies, We’re growing fast and looking for top talent to join our team. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

Camunda Hub is where enterprises run Camunda at scale. From Hub they manage their clusters and tenants, decide who can do what, and roll changes out across the whole organization. We’re looking for a Senior Product Builder to own the backend and architecture that let Hub manage a whole fleet of clusters. Your first focus will be the mechanism that sends access, secret, tenant and licence changes out to every connected cluster and brings deployments, health and audit records back. From there, you’ll shape Hub’s foundations: its event model, the service boundaries and data model other teams build on, and how Hub behaves when things fail or get upgraded. The platform is your product. Your users are platform administrators, Centres of Excellence and the builders across Camunda who depend on what you create. You’ll join a new two-person pod in our remote-first team. You’ll have real ownership as the person who makes the call, and you’ll build AI-first, from written specs through to production. Our customers run mission-critical processes on Camunda, so your work will matter to them every day.

Take over Hub's fleet propagation mechanism from the team building its first version, and make it work for access, secrets, tenants, licences and custom roles

Write short design documents for foundational changes, get them reviewed by the teams that depend on them, and turn them into tested, shipped code

Design how Hub behaves when things go wrong: offline clusters, partial failures, Hub outages, and clusters being adopted or detached

Build the path from clusters back to Hub, starting with deployment inventory, health, audit records and licence status

Design Hub's first event and webhook mechanism, so admins and agents can react to changes instead of polling for them

Join the pod's rotations and Hub on-call, and help other builders take on backend work through reviews, guidelines and contract tests

Experience building and running backend systems in production that other teams or customers depend on

Comfort taking a problem nobody owns yet and turning it into a design, a plan and shipped code

Product judgment for technical users: you talk to the people who use your APIs and make trade-offs on their behalf

Clear, short writing that lets a remote team review and decide asynchronously

Care for how a system behaves when parts of it fail, not only for the happy path

Designed and shipped a control plane or similar system that pushes desired state to many targets. Knows outbox or event-log patterns, idempotent apply, versioning, retries and drift reconciliation

Senior backend engineering in Java (API design, relational data models, multi-tenant SaaS services) and hands-on Helm and Kubernetes for self-managed installations

Software architecture and failure analysis: partial failure, blast radius, consistency trade-offs. Has debugged production incidents in a distributed system and is ready for on-call

Has shaped an API or platform as a product, and writes design docs other teams can build on. Gets agreement across teams without formal authority

Builds AI-first from written specifications and checks AI output as carefully as any other code

Kubernetes operators or GitOps tools, for example Argo CD

OIDC, SCIM and permission models, or secret managers and licence systems

Camunda 8 or another workflow or orchestration engine

Enough frontend skill to build a thin UI on top of a backend capability

#7165. Software Engineer - AI Tooling Infrastructure at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer, AI Tooling Infrastructure to join our AI Tooling team and help build and run the backend services behind Supabase’s AI products. The Supabase MCP server is one example of what the team runs in production, alongside the services that support agent skills and the dashboard assistant. You’ll work closely with the Platform, SRE, Auth, Billing and Edge Functions teams, and with the engineers on the AI Tooling team who build the product surfaces.

The AI Tooling team runs its own services in production and carries its own on-call. Traffic from AI clients and agents behaves differently from traffic from people: hundreds of calls a minute, retry loops, and no reading of deprecation warnings. This role makes sure the services they use are reliable, correctly authorized and observable. It is a systems and infrastructure role rather than a product AI role, and it is not an SRE role. You will build and operate these systems, not only advise on them.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments, owns what they ship in production, and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Build and operate the production backend services behind Supabase’s AI products, including the MCP server: deployment, scaling, configuration and release.

Take ownership of how those services are structured, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Define, measure and meet reliability targets for the services the team runs. Set SLOs with the SRE team, track error budgets, and use them to decide what to fix first.

Participate in the AI Tooling team on-call rotation, and be a first responder for incidents in those services. Investigate, fix, coordinate with other teams during incidents, and write the follow-ups and runbooks.

Work the AI Tooling team support queue alongside on-call, and turn recurring issues into fixes rather than repeated manual answers.

Own authentication and authorization for AI and agent clients: OAuth apps, scoped tokens, and permissions that make sense for agents as well as humans, including how we change or deprecate a permission without breaking existing integrations.

Own observability for the services the team runs: metrics, traces, logs, dashboards and alerts, so we find problems before customers do.

Handle capacity and abuse for agent traffic: rate limits, cost and safety controls, and behavior under load or when a dependency degrades.

Improve the architecture of the services the team owns as they grow, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Work with the Platform team on shared code, review ownership and release process, and with the SRE team on infrastructure, alerting and incident practice.

Contribute to the code of the AI Tooling team’s tools, such as the MCP server, where it affects reliability and security, in partnership with the engineers who own the product surface.

Have 5+ years of experience building and running backend services in production, and have carried on-call for them.

Write strong TypeScript or Go, and are comfortable working in the other.

Have designed and operated APIs with real consumers: versioning, error handling, rate limiting, and backwards-compatible change.

Know OAuth, OIDC, JWT and API authorization well enough to debug them in production and to explain where implementations commonly deviate from the spec.

Have a good understanding of PostgreSQL and relational databases.

Are comfortable with CI/CD, Docker and containerized deployments, and with infrastructure as code. Any tool is fine.

Can debug across services: follow a slow or failing request through several systems using metrics, traces and logs.

Communicate clearly in writing, work with a wide range of stakeholders, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly.

Pick your centre of gravity. We expect you to be deep in at least one of these, and to tell us which:

APIs and auth: You have designed permission models, token scopes or OAuth flows for third-party or machine clients, and you have migrated them without breaking users.

Operations: You have run production services yourself: carried the pager, written alerts and runbooks, used SLOs or similar to decide what to fix, and made shared systems less risky over time.

Gateways and distributed systems: You have built or run proxies or gateways, or debugged hard failures across services (retries, partial failures, upstream outages, fail-open vs fail-closed decisions), and you can reason about load and degradation.

Experience with agent tooling (MCP or similar), or serving traffic from AI agents and LLM clients.

Experience moving a service out of a larger codebase while keeping existing clients working.

Experience with Grafana, OpenTelemetry, Logflare or a similar observability stack, and real SLO work.

Open-source contributions, especially to Node.js, Go, TypeScript or API tooling.

Reproduce issues, create test cases and improve test coverage, if you have experience with testing.

Experienced in customer support, with 2+ years of experience providing billing support to technical customers.

Excellent communication skills and fluency in English.

Communicate clearly across both technical and non-technical audiences

Have experience in async or globally distributed teams

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

#7146. Senior Architect, Enterprise Agentic Automation at Camunda

Camunda is the enterprise platform for agentic orchestration, enabling organizations to coordinate AI agents, people, and systems across complex, end-to-end business processes. With built-in governance, auditability, and human oversight, Camunda gives enterprises the control they need to move AI from pilots to production — safely and at scale. Trusted by over 700 organizations worldwide, including 9 of top 10 US banks, Camunda helps enterprises boost operational efficiency, accelerate time-to-value, and deliver better customer experiences.Fully remote and global, we are in the middle of something bigger: transforming into an AI-first organisation, built on our own platform. We use Agentic AI to automate, orchestrate intelligent processes, and elevate human contribution across every team.Named GP Bullhound’s Top 100 Next Unicorn list, 2025 Great Place to Work certified. Visionary in 2025 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Business Orchestration and Automation Technologies. ranked 3rd in Flexa's 2026 Most Flexible Companies, We’re growing fast and looking for top talent to join our team. If you want meaningful work, visible impact and put something genuinely rare on your CV, keep reading.

We run Camunda on Camunda. Our Enterprise Agentic Automation Team (EAAT) builds production-grade agentic orchestration using Camunda's platform for Camunda's own operations, from Finance to Support to Go-to-Market. As Senior Architect, you own delivery on our EAAT-led initiatives end to end. You partner with process owners to uncover where the biggest value lies, redesign how the work should flow, decide where agents, rules or people handle each step, and shape it into scope our engineers can build from directly. Then you lead technical delivery management with our engineers, steering increments, dependencies and decisions until you get it live and prove the value. You won't design and hand over: you stay until it works in production, and keep evolving it.

Own delivery of our use cases end to end, business and technical, from discovery to production and value realization

Run discovery with process owners: as-is baseline, target process, and where agents, rules or humans handle each step

Turn target processes into epics engineers can build from directly: scenarios, acceptance criteria, increments

Design solutions within our reference architecture, and bring new patterns based on lessons learned

Lead technical delivery on your initiatives: plan increments with the engineers, manage dependencies and risks, and keep process owners and sponsors aligned on progress and decisions

Define and track value per initiative and increment, and keep business decisions moving

Feed delivery learnings back into the reference architecture, our delivery method and Camunda's product

Ability and/or willingness to use our product.

7+ years in solution architecture or technical/process consulting, with an engineering background (Java, JavaScript or similar) and significant time delivering for the business or customers

A track record of leading process orchestration initiatives (Camunda or comparable) from discovery to production, not just designing them and handing over

Strong business analysis and process management skills: runs discovery and process redesign workshops with business stakeholders, models processes in BPMN, and turns the outcome into solid solution designs

Solution and data architecture in an enterprise context: integrations and APIs, and the data a process and its agents need across systems

An AI-first mindset, including first experience designing and building enterprise-grade agentic solutions

Holds their own with senior stakeholders and gets decisions made

Experience in Finance or other back-office process domains and operations (P2P, Q2C)

Experience building value cases from operational data (baselines, volumes, value per increment), value engineering or benefits tracking

Experience with process intelligence or mining tools

Working knowledge of enterprise systems such as NetSuite, Salesforce or Zip
